<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### A decaying unit flow from every nearby slot

The topology gives routes for all lattice edges. Regularity now bounds their displacement, which will bound the number of routes using any one target edge.

<a id="stmt-prop-flow-decay"></a>
<div id="prop:flow-decay" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:flow-envelope"></a>
**Proposition 24** (Decay of a routed unit flow). *Let $d\ge3$, $\nu=1/(100d)$, and suppose the anchor $x$ is regular in the sense of Definition <a href="#def:regular-anchor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:regular-anchor">19</a>. Fix the local constant $M_*$ as in <a href="#eq:local-radius-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-radius-choice">[eq:local-radius-choice]</a> and take $R$ sufficiently large in terms of those fixed constants. For every available slot $a$ at any site $y\in B_R(x)$ there is a flow $J_a$ on the available-slot graph such that $$\label{eq:flow-envelope}
 \operatorname{div}J_a=\mathbf 1_{\{a\}},\qquad
 |J_a(e)|\le C(1+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(e,y))^{-k},
 \qquad k=d-1-d\nu.$$ In particular, $\sum_e J_a(e)^2<\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-flow-decay"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-flow-decay</summary>

<a id="eq:remote-hull-size"></a>
*Proof.* First we quantify the hull sizes. A blocked component meeting $B_{10R}(x)$ has fewer than $2d$ vertices. Otherwise, starting at a vertex there, a connected exploration would find $2d$ vertices within $2d-1$ component steps, all in a ball of radius $8(2d-1)\le M_*$, contradicting regularity. Such a component has diameter at most $8(2d-2)$, and its hull has diameter at most $$D_{\mathrm{loc}}=8(2d-2)+8.$$ For a component avoiding $B_{10R}(x)$, let $b_C=\min_{z\in C}|z-x|_\infty>10R$. Applying remote regularity at a minimizer gives $$\label{eq:remote-hull-size}
 \operatorname{diam}H_C\le 2(1+b_C)^\nu+8.$$ This also bounds the diameter of its coordinate bounding box. Since $\nu<1$, increasing a fixed lower threshold for $R$ makes the right side, plus any of the fixed enlargements used above, at most $b_C/10$. All sites of these enlarged boxes then have distance comparable to $b_C$ from every $y\in B_R(x)$. In particular, no such remote box contains $y$.

Apply the routes following <a href="#eq:once-only-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:once-only-map">[eq:once-only-map]</a>, using fixed choices of representatives and paths. The preceding size bounds show that the route of a lattice edge $e$ lies within distance <a id="eq:routing-displacement"></a>$$\label{eq:routing-displacement}
 C(1+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(e,y))^\nu$$ of that edge. For a local hull this is the fixed bound $D_{\mathrm{loc}}+2$. For each remote hull incident to $e$, an endpoint of $e$ belongs to its bounding box, so its $b_C$ is comparable to $1+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(e,y)$; now use <a href="#eq:remote-hull-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:remote-hull-size">[eq:remote-hull-size]</a>. The same reasoning applies when the edge joins two hulls. These comparisons are uniform over all injection sites $y\in B_R(x)$.

Choose one canonical available slot $c(z)$ at each unblocked site $z$. Lift every routed site path to these canonical slots. All its edges exist because every pair of available slots across a lattice edge is present. The map of the original lattice vertices into slots is $\psi(z)=c(\rho(z))$. Its preimages are finite: outside the finitely many finite hulls it is injective, and only those hulls can supply additional preimages.

We also need a bounded path from the specified slot $a$ to $\psi(y)$. If $y$ is in a hull, that hull is local and the separation between $y$ and $\rho(y)$ is at most $D_{\mathrm{loc}}+1$. Both sites are unblocked, so Lemma <a href="#lem:local-connectivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-connectivity">21</a> joins them through survivors in a fixed neighborhood of $y$. Its assumptions hold because $y\in B_R(x)$, the neighborhood has fixed radius, and $R$ dominates that radius. Lift this path, starting at $a$. If $\rho(y)=y$ but $a\ne c(y)$, use a two-edge path through a surviving neighbor of $y$. Such a neighbor exists, since blocking all $2d$ neighbors would violate local regularity. Thus in all cases there is a simple slot path $Q_a$ from $a$ to $\psi(y)$ of bounded length and displacement. The choice $K_{\rm conn}\ge8(2d-2)+12$ in the local-connectivity construction covers these requirements before $M_*$, $R$, and finally $p$ are fixed.

<a id="eq:orthant-flow"></a>
Start with an explicit unit flow $F_y$ on the full lattice. In the positive orthant based at $y$, give each site $y+n$, $n\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}^d$, $|n|_1=N$, mass $$a_N=\binom{N+d-1}{d-1}^{-1},$$ and send the fraction $(n_j+1)/(N+d)$ along its edge in direction $e_j$. Put zero on all other edges. At a site $y+n$ of level $N\ge1$, the incoming mass is $$a_{N-1}\frac{\sum_j n_j}{N+d-1}
 =a_{N-1}\frac{N}{N+d-1}=a_N,$$ which equals its outgoing mass. The outgoing mass at $y$ is one. Consequently $$\label{eq:orthant-flow}
 \operatorname{div}F_y=\mathbf 1_{\{y\}},\qquad
 |F_y(e)|\le C(1+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(e,y))^{1-d}.$$

<a id="eq:target-congestion"></a>
For each lattice edge, send its signed $F_y$-value along its lifted simple path. To justify this infinite sum and estimate it, fix a target slot edge $f$ and write $h=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(f,y)$. If the route of an original edge $e$ uses $f$, put $t=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(e,y)$. The displacement bound gives $$|h-t|\le C(1+t)^\nu+C.$$ Because $\nu<1$, this implies $1+t\asymp1+h$, with uniform constants: above a fixed threshold the error is at most $(1+t)/2$, and below that threshold all quantities are bounded. It follows also that $e$ lies within distance $C(1+h)^\nu$ of $f$. There are at most $$\label{eq:target-congestion}
 C(1+h)^{d\nu}$$ such lattice edges. Each routed path is simple, so any one of them uses $f$ at most once. Summing <a href="#eq:orthant-flow" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orthant-flow">[eq:orthant-flow]</a> over this finite set gives a well-defined flow $J_a^0$ with $$|J_a^0(f)|\le C(1+h)^{1-d+d\nu}.$$ The count <a href="#eq:target-congestion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:target-congestion">[eq:target-congestion]</a> already counts all contributions to $f$; no path-length factor is present.

Local finiteness of the path sum and finiteness of the vertex preimages justify the divergence calculation at any slot $b$: $$\begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{div}J_a^0(b)
 &=\sum_{e=(u,v)}F_y(e)
       \bigl(\mathbf 1_{\{\psi(u)=b\}}-\mathbf 1_{\{\psi(v)=b\}}\bigr)\\
 &=\sum_{z:\psi(z)=b}\operatorname{div}F_y(z)
 =\mathbf 1_{\{\psi(y)=b\}}.
\end{aligned}$$ Here one fixed orientation is used for each original lattice edge; the signed route is always from $\psi(u)$ to $\psi(v)$. Adding the unit path flow from $a$ to $\psi(y)$ therefore gives $$J_a=J_a^0+\mathbf 1_{Q_a},\qquad
 \operatorname{div}J_a=\mathbf 1_{\{a\}}.$$ The connector has fixed bounded support near $y$, so enlarging $C$ preserves the envelope <a href="#eq:flow-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flow-envelope">[eq:flow-envelope]</a>. Finally, $2k>d$ for $d\ge3$, and the number of slot edges at radius $h$ is at most $C(1+h)^{d-1}$. Squaring the envelope and summing proves finite energy. Equivalently, this is a unit flow to infinity: its net outward flux across any finite vertex set containing $a$ is one. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Overlap and arbitrary subunit injections

We now combine the individual flows. Their decay, rather than a disjointness property of their routes, controls their total energy.

<a id="stmt-lem-flow-overlap"></a>
<div id="lem:flow-overlap" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Overlap of two flows). *Suppose two flows on an available-slot graph obey $$|J(e)|\le C_0(1+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(e,y))^{-k},\qquad
 |J'(e)|\le C_0(1+\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(e,y'))^{-k},$$ where $d/2<k<d$. Then <a id="eq:flow-overlap"></a>$$\label{eq:flow-overlap}
 \sum_e |J(e)J'(e)|
 \le C(1+|y-y'|_\infty)^{-(2k-d)},$$ where $C$ depends only on $d,\ell,k,C_0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-flow-overlap"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-flow-overlap</summary>

*Proof.* Write $H=1+|y-y'|_\infty$. For bounded $H$ the assertion follows by summing the squared envelopes and Cauchy–Schwarz. Otherwise, in the ball of radius $(H-1)/3$ about either center, the other envelope is at most $CH^{-k}$, whereas the sum of the nearer envelope is at most $CH^{d-k}$. These two regions cost $CH^{d-2k}$. In the remaining region the two distances are comparable. Dyadic annuli of radii comparable to $2^jH$, $j\ge0$, cost at most $$C\sum_{j\ge0}(2^jH)^{d-2k}\le CH^{d-2k},$$ since $2k>d$. Changing a site distance to the distance from an incident edge changes it by at most one, and the number of slot edges per site is bounded by $2d\ell^2$. Thus the same estimates apply to the stated edge sum. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-flow-energy"></a>
<div id="prop:flow-energy" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:injection-flow-energy"></a>
<a id="eq:injection-pairing"></a>
**Proposition 26** (Energy for arbitrary injections). *Let $d\ge3$, $\alpha=1/100$, and $\nu=1/(100d)$. Fix $M_*$ as in <a href="#eq:local-radius-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-radius-choice">[eq:local-radius-choice]</a> and take $R$ sufficiently large as in Proposition <a href="#prop:flow-decay" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flow-decay">24</a>. Let $T$ be a finite set of $m$ anchor slots with sites $x_i$, satisfying $$\#\{i\in T:x_i\in B_h(z)\}\le L(1+h)^\alpha
 \quad\text{for all }z\in\mathbb Z^d,\ h\ge0.$$ Fix an available-slot graph and a subset $I\subset T$ of anchors regular for this graph. For each $i\in I$, let $\mu_i$ be any nonnegative mass on its slots, supported over $B_R(x_i)$ and satisfying $\sum_a\mu_i(a)\le1$. There is a flow $J$ such that $$\label{eq:injection-flow-energy}
 \operatorname{div}J=\sum_{i\in I}\mu_i,
 \qquad \sum_e J(e)^2\le C|I|R^\alpha\le CmR^\alpha.$$ Consequently every finitely supported real function $g$ on the graph satisfies $$\label{eq:injection-pairing}
 \left|\left\langle\sum_{i\in I}\mu_i,g\right\rangle\right|
 \le \bigl(CmR^\alpha\mathcal D(g)\bigr)^{1/2},
 \qquad \mathcal D(g)=\frac12\sum_e(g(e^-)-g(e^+))^2.$$ All constants are uniform over the choices of $I$ and of the masses.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-flow-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-flow-energy</summary>

*Proof.* The empty case uses $J=0$. Otherwise, for each slot $a$ in the support of $\mu_i$, take a unit flow $J_{i,a}$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:flow-decay" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flow-decay">24</a>, using anchor $x_i$, and set $$J=\sum_{i\in I}\sum_a\mu_i(a)J_{i,a}.$$ This is a finite linear combination, and its divergence is as asserted. Put $q=2k-d=d-2-2d\nu>\alpha$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:flow-overlap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:flow-overlap">25</a>, the absolute overlap of $J_{i,a}$ and $J_{j,b}$ is at most $C(1+|y_a-y_b|_\infty)^{-q}$, where $y_a,y_b$ are the sites of their injection slots. If $|x_i-x_j|_\infty>4R$, then $|y_a-y_b|_\infty\ge |x_i-x_j|_\infty/2$, uniformly over both supports. For the remaining anchor pairs use the uniform constant overlap bound. The mass bound of one per anchor therefore gives $$\sum_eJ(e)^2\le C\sum_{i\in I}
 \left[\#\{j\in I:|x_j-x_i|_\infty\le4R\}
 +\sum_{\substack{j\in I\\|x_j-x_i|_\infty>4R}}
     (1+|x_j-x_i|_\infty)^{-q}\right].$$ The first term is at most $CL R^\alpha$. Splitting the second into annuli $2^j4R<|x_j-x_i|_\infty\le2^{j+1}4R$ bounds it by $$CL\sum_{j\ge0}(2^jR)^{\alpha-q}\le C,$$ because $R\ge1$ and $q>\alpha$. This proves <a href="#eq:injection-flow-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:injection-flow-energy">[eq:injection-flow-energy]</a>, including repeated anchor sites since the growth assumption counts slots.

Finally, finite support of $g$ permits summation by divergence without a term at infinity: $$\left\langle\sum_i\mu_i,g\right\rangle
 =\sum_e J(e)(g(e^-)-g(e^+)).$$ Cauchy–Schwarz and $\sum_e|\nabla_e g|^2=2\mathcal D(g)$ give <a href="#eq:injection-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:injection-pairing">[eq:injection-pairing]</a>. In particular it applies to functions that vanish on the killed boundary and outside the physical box, with exactly the energy normalization in <a href="#eq:slot-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:slot-energy">[eq:slot-energy]</a>. ◻

</details>

The construction is deterministic at a single slice. Thus the masses and the set of regular anchors may depend on the whole exposed environment; the pairing bound requires neither independence nor a choice of flows continuous in time.

<a id="sec:leakage"></a>
### Heat-flow leakage and the pin bootstrap

We now use the geometric estimates to bound the return fraction in the simultaneous pin test. The main task is to prove that the hitting function loses nearly $m$ units of squared norm during each of two short portions of a period, where $m$ is the number of new pins. Local diffusion spreads the mass on those pins, and the flow estimate controls its pairing with the rest of the hitting function. Averaging the accumulated heat dissipation will absorb this pairing. The Nash method for Markov semigroups is developed in (Diaconis and Saloff-Coste 1996, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[DiaconisSaloffCoste1996](#ref-DiaconisSaloffCoste1996)]. The proof below accounts explicitly for the changing hole sets, killing, instantaneous contractions, and the restriction of the Nash inequality to regular times.

<a id="eq:leakage-growth"></a>
Throughout the section, $d\ge3$, $\ell=2S$, and $\Lambda$ is a finite cube. We use the parameters of Section <a href="#sec:sparse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sparse">5</a>, namely $$\alpha=\frac1{100},\qquad \nu=\frac1{100d},\qquad
 a=\frac{11}{10},\qquad s=p^{-a},\qquad R=\lceil C_*s\rceil,
 \qquad \beta\ge5s.$$ The spatial growth constant $L$, the regularity radius $M_*$, and the constant $C_*$ will be fixed independently of the final choice of $p$. The test $T$ consists of $m\ge1$ distinct slot points at one time, is disjoint from $E\supseteq D_0$, and satisfies $$\label{eq:leakage-growth}
 \#\{i\in T:x_i\in B_h(z)\}\le L(1+h)^\alpha
 \quad(z\in\mathbb Z^d,\ h\ge0).$$ Here $x_i$ denotes the site of the slot point $i$. Write $P=E\cup T$, and sample the exposed paths under $\mathbb P_P$, as in Proposition <a href="#prop:exposure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:exposure">12</a>. Expectations of quantities determined by these paths use their actual marginal, including the factor $g_P$ in <a href="#eq:exposure-outer-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exposure-outer-law">[eq:exposure-outer-law]</a>. At a fixed exposure, the propagators use the unconditioned fresh-turn walk. As in Proposition <a href="#prop:pin-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pin-test">6</a>, $r$ is the expected fraction of points of $T$ whose next pin along the actual cycle again belongs to $T$.

<a id="eq:leakage-hitting-input"></a>
Recall the one-period, column-forward kernel $U$ of the fresh-turn walk, killed on visiting $D_0$. At the cut containing $T$, the function $f$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:return-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:return-energy">16</a> is $1$ on $T$ and, off $T$, is the probability of a future visit to $T$ before killing, namely the minimal nonnegative hitting solution. In particular, $$\label{eq:leakage-hitting-input}
 0\le f\le1,\qquad f|_{\partial\Lambda}=0,\qquad
 U^*f=f\ \hbox{off }T,\qquad
 m(1-r)\ge\mathbb E_P\langle f,(I-U)f\rangle.$$ The last inequality incorporates the conditional first-moment identity of Proposition <a href="#prop:first-moment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:first-moment">14</a> and the relaxation of the other pins of $E$. A class of the relaxed walk that never reaches $T$ has $f=0$; certain absorption is unnecessary. In particular $f$ is a function of the exposed paths, and we will not require it to be independent of any geometric event. All the propagators below are measurable functions of finite-jump data. The hitting function is measurable as the increasing limit of finite-horizon hitting probabilities, so the expectations used below are well defined.

<a id="eq:leakage-target"></a>
For grid pin sets satisfying the larger-pin induction hypothesis $\mathsf I_p(P)$ of <a href="#eq:induction-assertion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:induction-assertion">[eq:induction-assertion]</a>, our aim is $$\label{eq:leakage-target}
 \mathbb E_P\langle f,(I-U)f\rangle\ge m[1-\varepsilon(p)],
 \qquad \varepsilon(p)=o(p).$$ Together with <a href="#eq:leakage-hitting-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-hitting-input">[eq:leakage-hitting-input]</a>, this gives $r\le\varepsilon(p)$. The simultaneous pin test then recovers $\mathbb P_E(T\subseteq\mathcal B_E)\le p^m$ once $2\varepsilon(p)\le p$, closing the induction step. To obtain <a href="#eq:leakage-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-target">[eq:leakage-target]</a>, split $f=\eta+g$, where $\eta=\mathbf 1_T$ and $g=f-\eta$. Then $0\le g\le1$ and $\langle\eta,g\rangle=0$. We first relate the quadratic form to norm losses at both ends of the period. The Nash estimate will make the propagated $\eta$ small in squared norm, while the flow estimate will control its pairing with the propagated $g$ through the square root of that function’s energy at the same time.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Ordered propagation and the two ends of a period

<a id="eq:leakage-generator"></a>
We first fix one exposed environment. Between its finitely many instantaneous changes, the surviving interior holes carry the symmetric rate-$1/2$ heat equation. A boundary hole is assigned value zero. Thus the generator on an interior hole $k$ is $$\label{eq:leakage-generator}
 (L_t v)_k=\frac12\sum_{j:\{j,k\}\text{ is an available slot edge}}
                   (\widetilde v_j-v_k),$$ where $\widetilde v$ equals $v$ on interior holes and zero on boundary holes. The diagonal includes the escape rates to the boundary. This is continuous killing throughout the heat interval.

<a id="eq:ordered-killing"></a>
At an instantaneous change, let $F$ be the forced bijection or the average of the allowed within-site completion bijections, from the hole set just before the change to that just after it. If $R_-$ and $R_+$ are the projections deleting killed coordinates on those two sets, respectively, the killed factor is $$\label{eq:ordered-killing}
             R_+FR_-,\qquad
             (R_+FR_-)^*=R_-F^*R_+.$$ The projections remain in this order. For heat intervals, the corresponding assertion follows either from the symmetric Dirichlet generator or by transposing the sampled surviving paths.

By the subpermutation argument in <a href="#eq:hole-contractions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hole-contractions">[eq:hole-contractions]</a>, these matrices, their products, and their adjoints are nonnegative, have row and column sums at most one, and contract the counting $\ell^2$ norm. We will also use the two sum bounds separately: the row bound gives $0\le Av\le1$ when $0\le v\le1$, while the column bound gives $\|Av\|_1\le\|v\|_1$ for $v\ge0$.

Put the time of $T$ at the beginning and end of a period. Let $C_u$ be the initial propagation of duration $u$, and let $C_u^{\rm rev}$ be the adjoint of the ending propagation of duration $u$. Forward propagation includes the changes after its initial cut through its final cut, under the post-completion convention at time zero. Reverse propagation traverses precisely those factors in the opposite order. If the ending cut is a completion cut, its adjoint completion is the first factor traversed in reverse time. It is included exactly once, including when it occurs immediately at elapsed time zero. We set $C_0=C_0^{\rm rev}=I$ on the initial, already unkilled coordinates and count any such initial reverse jump in every positive duration that traverses it. This convention allows a contraction jump at $u=0+$.

<a id="stmt-lem-two-ends"></a>
<div id="lem:two-ends" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:two-ends"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Leakage at both ends). *For every fixed exposed environment, $s>0$, and period $\beta\ge4s$, the preceding killed propagators satisfy, for every real vector $v$ on the holes at the distinguished cut with zero boundary values, $$\label{eq:two-ends}
 \langle v,(I-U)v\rangle\ge\frac12\left(
   \|v\|_2^2-\|C_{2s}v\|_2^2
  +\|v\|_2^2-\|C_{2s}^{\rm rev}v\|_2^2\right).$$ Both $u\mapsto\|C_uv\|_2$ and $u\mapsto\|C_u^{\rm rev}v\|_2$ are nonincreasing.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-two-ends"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-two-ends</summary>

*Proof.* The nonoverlapping initial and ending portions factor $U=BMA$, with $A=C_{2s}$, $B^*=C_{2s}^{\rm rev}$, and $M$ the intervening propagation. At $\beta=4s$, assign every shared-cut jump once according to the preceding convention and take the intervening identity on that cut. All factors are contractions, so $$\langle v,Uv\rangle
 =\langle B^*v,MAv\rangle
 \le\|B^*v\|_2\|Av\|_2
 \le\tfrac12\bigl(\|B^*v\|_2^2+\|Av\|_2^2\bigr).$$ This proves <a href="#eq:two-ends" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-ends">[eq:two-ends]</a>. Longer initial portions are obtained by left multiplication by further contraction factors. For the ending adjoints the same fact follows after reversing the factor order, using <a href="#eq:ordered-killing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ordered-killing">[eq:ordered-killing]</a>. It proves both monotonicity assertions. ◻

</details>

The reverse construction has symmetric heat between changes, inverse forced transports, averages of inverse completion bijections, and the ordered killing projections. Thus it has the same deterministic analytic properties as forward propagation. Its environment is the original exposed environment read in reverse order; no invariance of the environment law under time reversal is asserted or needed.

Thus <a href="#eq:leakage-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-target">[eq:leakage-target]</a> will follow if each of these two propagations loses at least $m[1-\varepsilon(p)]$ in expected squared norm by time $2s$. We prove both estimates through the same argument.

For the rest of the estimates, $C_u$ denotes either of the two propagations, and $t(u)$ denotes its physical time slice modulo $\beta$. On the available slot graph, extended by all slots outside $\Lambda$, use the energy $$\mathcal D_{t}(v)=\frac12\sum_{\{j,k\}\text{ available at }t}
                          (v_j-v_k)^2.$$ Vectors propagated in the box are extended by zero on its boundary and outside. Since the boundary is the inner boundary, any edge crossing out of the box has two zero values.

<a id="eq:loss-lower"></a>
Write $\eta_u=C_u\eta$, $g_u=C_ug$, and $\mathcal L(u)=\|f\|_2^2-\|C_uf\|_2^2$. For a jump traversed at elapsed time $v$, let $$\Delta_g(v)=\|g_{v,\mathrm{before}}\|_2^2
                  -\|g_{v,\mathrm{after}}\|_2^2\ge0.$$ Here “before” and “after” refer to the chosen direction of propagation. Denote the finite list of jumps traversed by duration $u$ by $\mathcal J(u)$, including an initial reverse jump when present. Between jumps, <a href="#eq:leakage-generator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-generator">[eq:leakage-generator]</a> gives $$\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm du}\|g_u\|_2^2
     =-2\mathcal D_{t(u)}(g_u).$$ To check the normalization, an interior–interior edge contributes $-(g_j-g_k)^2$ to this derivative, and an interior–boundary edge contributes $-g_j^2$. These are exactly the contributions to $-2\mathcal D$. Summing continuous and instantaneous losses and then expanding $\|\eta_u+g_u\|_2^2$ yields the exact identity <a id="eq:exact-loss"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal L(u)
 &=m-\|\eta_u\|_2^2-2\langle\eta_u,g_u\rangle\notag\\
 &\quad+2\int_0^u\mathcal D_{t(v)}(g_v)\,\mathrm dv
       +\sum_{v\in\mathcal J(u)}\Delta_g(v).
       \label{eq:exact-loss}
\end{aligned}$$ The sum includes a completion at an endpoint whenever that completion is a factor of the propagation. Dropping it gives $$\label{eq:loss-lower}
 \mathcal L(u)\ge
 m-\|\eta_u\|_2^2-2\langle\eta_u,g_u\rangle
       +2\int_0^u\mathcal D_{t(v)}(g_v)\,\mathrm dv.$$ Keeping this as an inequality is necessary even when a completion is the only nontrivial instantaneous operation.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Localization with actual and fresh marks

<a id="eq:local-pieces"></a>
For $i\in T$, let $\eta_{i,u}$ be the propagation from unit mass at $i$, with the additional rule that a path is killed when its site leaves $B_R(x_i)$. Use this additional killing for continuous moves and on both sides of instantaneous changes. Positivity and the path description give $$\label{eq:local-pieces}
 0\le\zeta_u:=\sum_{i\in T}\eta_{i,u}\le\eta_u\le1,
 \qquad \|\eta_{i,u}\|_1\le1.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-localization"></a>
<div id="lem:localization" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:localization"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Localization averaged over the pin law). *Fix $d$ and $\ell$. There is a constant $C_*=C_*(d,\ell)$ such that for $s\ge1$, $R=\lceil C_*s\rceil$, $\beta\ge5s$, every finite cube, every $E\supseteq D_0$ consisting of $D_0$ and finitely many pins, and every disjoint single-time test $T$, one has $$\label{eq:localization}
 \mathbb E_P\|\eta_u-\zeta_u\|_1\le C m e^{-cs}
       \qquad(0\le u\le2s)$$ for either direction of propagation. The constants are independent of the volume, the pin sets, $\beta$, and $s$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-localization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-localization</summary>

*Proof.* First sample a complete actual exchange picture with law $\mathbb P_P$ and expose its missing cycles. Conditional on that exposure, realize the unconditioned one-particle propagation of Proposition <a href="#prop:exposure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:exposure">12</a> using independent fresh rate-$1/2$ Poisson clocks on all slot edges, accepting a fresh mark only when its two endpoints are holes. The forced moves use marks of the actual picture. Completion randomness changes slots only within a site. This construction is for the unconditioned fresh walk; its exposure still has the marginal induced by the full pin law.

The difference in <a href="#eq:local-pieces" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-pieces">[eq:local-pieces]</a>, summed over output slots, is bounded by the sum of the probabilities that these individual walks exit their respective balls before elapsed time $u$. Exiting requires at least $R$ nearest-neighbor spatial steps. The first $R$ such steps give a chronological list of marks, each from either the actual picture or the fresh family. For a fixed list of sites, slot edges, and choices of source, the actual marks used are distinct: the time interval has length at most $2s<\beta$, so its image on the time circle does not use a mark twice. This remains true if the interval crosses time zero, and if it is traversed backwards. Fresh marks in the list are distinct as well.

Suppose a source choice uses $k$ actual marks and $R-k$ fresh marks. By Lemma <a href="#lem:factorial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:factorial">5</a>, the joint factorial measure of the actual marks is bounded by $2^k$ times their base product intensity. Independence of the fresh family then bounds the mixed measure by $$1^k(1/2)^{R-k}\,\mathrm dt_1\cdots\mathrm dt_R$$ on the prescribed edge coordinates. All source choices are integrated over the same ordered simplex $0<t_1<\cdots<t_R<u$, of volume $u^R/R!$. At each spatial step there are at most $2d\ell^2$ choices for the next site and the slot edge, even if one ignores constraints from the current slot. Summing source choices and edge choices therefore bounds the expected number of such lists, for one initial point, by <a id="eq:mixed-factorial-count"></a>$$\label{eq:mixed-factorial-count}
 (2d\ell^2)^R(1+1/2)^R\frac{u^R}{R!}
       \le\frac{(2Ks)^R}{R!},\qquad K=3d\ell^2.$$ The existence of an exit path is bounded by this count; the counted lists need not themselves be realizable walk histories. In particular, the restrictions imposed by the exposure can be ignored when taking this upper bound. No factorial bound conditional on a fixed exposure has been used.

Since $R!\ge(R/e)^R$, choosing $C_*>4eK$ bounds the last expression by $2^{-R}\le e^{-cs}$. Sum over $i\in T$ to obtain <a href="#eq:localization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:localization">[eq:localization]</a>. Transposition merely reverses the chronological lists and the forced transports. The same mixed factorial estimate applies under the original law $\mathbb P_P$, which proves the reverse assertion too. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Diffusion during regular times

<a id="eq:leakage-delta"></a>
<a id="eq:leakage-nash-input"></a>
<a id="eq:leakage-flow-input"></a>
We now use the downward-induction hypothesis only through the three proved inputs from Sections <a href="#sec:sparse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sparse">5</a> and <a href="#sec:flows" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:flows">6</a>. Assume $\mathsf I_p(P)$, as defined in <a href="#eq:induction-assertion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:induction-assertion">[eq:induction-assertion]</a>. With the fixed choices of constants there, Proposition <a href="#prop:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:regularity">20</a> gives $$\label{eq:leakage-delta}
 \mathbb P_P(t\text{ is not regular for }i)
       \le\delta:=C R^d p^{2d}+C e^{-cR^\xi}
       \quad(i\in T, t\in[0,\beta)),$$ where $\xi>0$ and the constants are fixed for all $p$ below a preliminary threshold. At a regular slice, Proposition <a href="#prop:local-nash" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:local-nash">22</a> states $$\label{eq:leakage-nash-input}
 \|v\|_2^{2+4/d}\le C\mathcal D_t(v)\|v\|_1^{4/d}$$ for nonnegative $v$ supported on surviving slots above $B_R(x_i)$, with zero extension. Finally, Proposition <a href="#prop:flow-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flow-energy">26</a> applies to any subset of the anchors regular at that same time and to any nonnegative injections of mass at most one per anchor supported above its $R$-ball. Their sum is the divergence of a flow $J$ with $$\label{eq:leakage-flow-input}
                   \sum_e J(e)^2\le C mR^\alpha.$$ The flow estimate follows from Proposition <a href="#prop:flow-decay" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flow-decay">24</a> and the growth condition; its applicability to arbitrary injection points and subunit masses is part of its statement.

<a id="stmt-lem-regular-time-decay"></a>
<div id="lem:regular-time-decay" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:piece-decay"></a>
<a id="eq:all-pieces-decay"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Decay after enough regular time). *Let $T$ satisfy <a href="#eq:leakage-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-growth">[eq:leakage-growth]</a>, and assume $\mathsf I_p(P)$ for $P=E\cup T$, with $p$ below the preliminary threshold in Proposition <a href="#prop:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:regularity">20</a>. For the localized propagators defined above, in either direction and for $s\le u\le2s$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_P\|\eta_{i,u}\|_2^2
       &\le C(s^{-d/2}+\delta)\quad(i\in T),
                    \label{eq:piece-decay}\\
 \mathbb E_P\|\eta_u\|_2^2
       &\le C m\bigl[e^{-cs}+R^\alpha(s^{-d/2}+\delta)\bigr].
                    \label{eq:all-pieces-decay}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-regular-time-decay"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-regular-time-decay</summary>

*Proof.* Put $q_i(u)=\|\eta_{i,u}\|_2^2$. All localized factors are contractions, so $0\le q_i\le1$ and $q_i$ is nonincreasing, including at jumps. Between jumps the additional killing gives the Dirichlet generator with zero values outside $B_R(x_i)$ as well as on the physical boundary. Consequently, at every regular smooth time, <a href="#eq:leakage-nash-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-nash-input">[eq:leakage-nash-input]</a> and $\|\eta_{i,u}\|_1\le1$ imply <a id="eq:nash-differential"></a>$$\label{eq:nash-differential}
 q_i'(u)=-2\mathcal D_{t(u)}(\eta_{i,u})
                         \le-c q_i(u)^{1+2/d}.$$ At other smooth times $q_i'\le0$. As long as $q_i>0$, the function $q_i^{-2/d}$ thus increases at a fixed positive rate during regular time and cannot decrease at other times or jumps. If $q_i$ reaches zero, it stays zero. It follows in either case that $$\int_0^s\mathbf 1_{\{t(v)\text{ regular for }i\}}\,\mathrm dv\ge s/2
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 q_i(u)\le C s^{-d/2}\quad(s\le u\le2s).$$ The expected amount of nonregular time in $[0,s]$ is at most $s\delta$ by <a href="#eq:leakage-delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-delta">[eq:leakage-delta]</a> and Fubini. Markov’s inequality bounds the probability of its exceeding $s/2$ by $2\delta$. This proves <a href="#eq:piece-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:piece-decay">[eq:piece-decay]</a>. For reverse propagation, $t(v)$ simply runs in the opposite direction; the same fixed-time estimate <a href="#eq:leakage-delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-delta">[eq:leakage-delta]</a> gives the same integral bound, without changing the probability law.

At any slot above $z$, only anchors with $x_i\in B_R(z)$ can contribute to $\zeta_u$. Their number is at most $L(1+R)^\alpha\le C R^\alpha$ by <a href="#eq:leakage-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-growth">[eq:leakage-growth]</a>. Hence pointwise Cauchy–Schwarz and summation give $$\|\zeta_u\|_2^2\le C R^\alpha\sum_{i\in T}q_i(u).$$ Since $0\le\zeta_u\le\eta_u\le1$, $$\|\eta_u\|_2^2-\|\zeta_u\|_2^2
       =\sum_k(\eta_u(k)-\zeta_u(k))(\eta_u(k)+\zeta_u(k))
       \le2\|\eta_u-\zeta_u\|_1.$$ Use Lemma <a href="#lem:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:localization">28</a> and <a href="#eq:piece-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:piece-decay">[eq:piece-decay]</a> to obtain <a href="#eq:all-pieces-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:all-pieces-decay">[eq:all-pieces-decay]</a>. ◻

</details>

The initial pin mass has now spread sufficiently in mean square. The remaining term in <a href="#eq:loss-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:loss-lower">[eq:loss-lower]</a> is its pairing with $g_u$. We next use flows at the very slice where this pairing occurs, so that the positive heat energy can absorb it.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Absorbing the interaction at the same time

<a id="stmt-prop-leakage"></a>
<div id="prop:leakage" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:epsilon-definition"></a>
**Proposition 30** (Expected leakage). *Fix the constants in the preceding geometric estimates and choose $C_*$ as in Lemma <a href="#lem:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:localization">28</a>. For all sufficiently small $p>0$, every finite cube, every circular grid of mesh at most $p^{4d}$, every $E$ consisting of $D_0$ and grid pins, and every disjoint single-time grid test $T$ satisfying <a href="#eq:leakage-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-growth">[eq:leakage-growth]</a>, assume $\mathsf I_p(P)$ for $P=E\cup T$. If $\beta\ge5s$, then, for each of the forward and reverse propagations, <a id="eq:expected-leakage"></a>$$\label{eq:expected-leakage}
 \mathbb E_P\bigl[\|f\|_2^2-\|C_{2s}f\|_2^2\bigr]
             \ge m[1-\varepsilon(p)],$$ where one may take $$\label{eq:epsilon-definition}
 \varepsilon(p)=C\left[e^{-cs}
       +R^\alpha\left(s^{-d/2}+\delta+s^{-1}\right)\right],
 \qquad \delta=C R^d p^{2d}+C e^{-cR^\xi}.$$ All constants are independent of the volume, period, grid, pin sets, and final $p$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-leakage"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-leakage</summary>

<a id="eq:discarded-pairing"></a>
*Proof.* For $s\le u\le2s$, let $$\mu_u=\sum_{i:\,t(u)\text{ regular for }i}\eta_{i,u}.$$ By <a href="#eq:local-pieces" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-pieces">[eq:local-pieces]</a>, $0\le\mu_u\le\eta_u$. The nonnegative discarded mass is bounded by the localization error plus the masses attached to nonregular anchors. Since $0\le g_u\le1$ and each local mass is at most one, Lemma <a href="#lem:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:localization">28</a> and <a href="#eq:leakage-delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-delta">[eq:leakage-delta]</a> give $$\label{eq:discarded-pairing}
 \mathbb E_P\langle\eta_u-\mu_u,g_u\rangle
                         \le C m(e^{-cs}+\delta).$$ This uses only pointwise bounds, so dependence of $g_u$ on the same environment causes no change.

At a fixed regular slice, each summand of $\mu_u$ meets exactly the support and mass hypotheses of <a href="#eq:leakage-flow-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-flow-input">[eq:leakage-flow-input]</a>. Choose a reference orientation for the available edges, with $\operatorname{div}J$ equal to outgoing minus incoming flow. Since $g_u$ is finitely supported, summation by parts has finitely many nonzero terms and gives $$\langle\mu_u,g_u\rangle
   =\sum_{e=(j,k)}J(e)(g_u(j)-g_u(k)).$$ Cauchy–Schwarz, <a href="#eq:leakage-flow-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-flow-input">[eq:leakage-flow-input]</a>, and $\sum_e|\nabla_e g_u|^2=2\mathcal D_{t(u)}(g_u)$ imply the deterministic inequality <a id="eq:same-time-pairing"></a>$$\label{eq:same-time-pairing}
       |\langle\mu_u,g_u\rangle|
          \le\bigl(A\mathcal D_{t(u)}(g_u)\bigr)^{1/2},
               \qquad A=C m R^\alpha.$$ It suffices that a flow exists at each time: the resulting inequality contains only measurable propagated vectors and energies, so no measurable or continuous choice of flows is required.

To make the remainder in weighted Young’s inequality integrable at the final time, use an averaging density that vanishes linearly there; its tail weight then vanishes quadratically. Average <a href="#eq:loss-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:loss-lower">[eq:loss-lower]</a> over $u\in[s,2s]$ with probability density $$w(u)=\frac{2(2s-u)}{s^2},\qquad
 W(u)=\int_u^{2s}w(v)\,\mathrm dv=\frac{(2s-u)^2}{s^2}.$$ Since $\mathcal L$ is nondecreasing, $\mathcal L(2s)\ge\int_s^{2s}w(u)\mathcal L(u)\,\mathrm du$. Tonelli’s theorem shows that the positive energy in this average contains $$2\int_s^{2s}W(u)\mathcal D_{t(u)}(g_u)\,\mathrm du;$$ the additional contribution from $[0,s]$ is nonnegative. At each $s\le u<2s$, weighted Young’s inequality gives <a id="eq:weighted-young"></a>$$\label{eq:weighted-young}
 2w(u)\sqrt{A\mathcal D_{t(u)}(g_u)}
 \le2W(u)\mathcal D_{t(u)}(g_u)+\frac{A w(u)^2}{2W(u)}.$$ The same-time energy has therefore absorbed the retained pairing. The remaining integral is finite, because <a id="eq:weight-integral"></a>$$\label{eq:weight-integral}
                \int_s^{2s}\frac{w(u)^2}{W(u)}\,\mathrm du=\frac4s.$$ The endpoint $u=2s$ has measure zero; the quotient has the constant value $4/s^2$ on $[s,2s)$.

Taking expectation, using <a href="#eq:discarded-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discarded-pairing">[eq:discarded-pairing]</a>, and then Lemma <a href="#lem:regular-time-decay" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:regular-time-decay">29</a>, we conclude that $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_P\mathcal L(2s)
 &\ge m
  -C m\bigl[e^{-cs}+R^\alpha(s^{-d/2}+\delta)\bigr]
  -C m(e^{-cs}+\delta)-C mR^\alpha/s\\
 &\ge m\left[1-C\left(e^{-cs}
                   +R^\alpha(s^{-d/2}+\delta+s^{-1})\right)\right].
\end{aligned}$$ Here $R\ge1$ allows the unweighted $\delta$ error to be included in $R^\alpha\delta$. Every step applied to either ordered propagation, which proves both assertions. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Choosing the constants and closing the finite induction

<a id="eq:epsilon-small"></a>
The error has enough room to recover the assumed sparsity with the same parameter $p$. Indeed, after $C_*$ is fixed, $R$ is comparable to $s=p^{-a}$ for $p\le1$. The nonexponential errors $R^\alpha/s$, $R^\alpha s^{-d/2}$, and $R^{\alpha+d}p^{2d}$ in <a href="#eq:epsilon-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:epsilon-definition">[eq:epsilon-definition]</a> are bounded by constant multiples of <a id="eq:leakage-exponents"></a>$$\label{eq:leakage-exponents}
 p^{a(1-\alpha)},\qquad
 p^{a(d/2-\alpha)},\qquad
 p^{2d-a(d+\alpha)},$$ respectively. Their exponents satisfy $$a(1-\alpha)=\frac{1089}{1000}>1,\qquad
 a(d/2-\alpha)\ge\frac{1639}{1000}>1,\qquad
 2d-a(d+\alpha)\ge\frac{2689}{1000}>1.$$ The last two lower bounds use $d\ge3$; each exponent increases with $d$. Both $e^{-cs}$ and $R^\alpha e^{-cR^\xi}$ are smaller than every fixed power of $p$. Thus $$\label{eq:epsilon-small}
                         \varepsilon(p)=o(p)\quad(p\downarrow0).$$

For clarity, the constant choices can be made in the following order. Fix $d,\ell,\alpha,\nu,a$. Fix the witness scale $b$ and then its growth constant $L\ge\max\{2d,\ell,4\}$ as in Lemma <a href="#lem:component-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:component-tail">18</a>. Fix the local connectivity radii and a sufficiently large $M_*$ for Proposition <a href="#prop:local-nash" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:local-nash">22</a> and the hull and flow constructions of Lemma <a href="#lem:hulls" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hulls">23</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:flow-decay" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:flow-decay">24</a>. Fix a preliminary $p_0>0$ that absorbs the admissible witness count in Lemma <a href="#lem:component-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:component-tail">18</a>; retain the resulting fixed constants in Proposition <a href="#prop:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:regularity">20</a>, including $c$ and $\xi$. Next choose $C_*$ for Lemma <a href="#lem:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:localization">28</a>. Finally reduce $p\le p_0$ so that $R$ exceeds the fixed local radii and the fixed lower bounds needed for the flow comparisons, $p<1/4$, and $2\varepsilon(p)\le p$. The limit <a href="#eq:epsilon-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:epsilon-small">[eq:epsilon-small]</a> permits all of these final requirements simultaneously. No constant in the component tail is recomputed using this final $p$.

<a id="stmt-thm-pin-bootstrap"></a>
<div id="thm:pin-bootstrap" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:singleton-missing"></a>
**Theorem 31** (Uniform pin estimate). *For every integer $d\ge3$ and integer slot multiplicity $\ell\ge1$, there exist $L\ge\max\{2d,\ell,4\}$ and $p_*\in(0,1/4)$ with the following property. Let $0<p\le p_*$ and $\beta\ge5p^{-11/10}$. In any finite cube $\Lambda$, choose a finite circular time grid containing zero and of mesh at most $p^{4d}$. Let $E$ consist of $D_0$ and any subset of the slot points on this grid. Then every nonempty test $T$ at a single grid time, disjoint from $E$ and satisfying <a href="#eq:leakage-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-growth">[eq:leakage-growth]</a>, obeys <a id="eq:bootstrap-closed"></a>$$\label{eq:bootstrap-closed}
                  \mathbb P_E(T\subseteq\mathcal B_E)\le p^{|T|}.$$ In particular, with $p=p_*$ and $\beta_0=5p_*^{-11/10}<\infty$, for every finite $\beta\ge\beta_0$, every cube, and every slot $j$ at time zero, $$\label{eq:singleton-missing}
                  \mathbb P_{D_0}(j\in\mathcal B_{D_0})\le p_*.$$ The constants depend only on $d$ and $\ell$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-pin-bootstrap"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-pin-bootstrap</summary>

*Proof.* Fix all auxiliary constants in the stated order and choose $p_*$ so that every $0<p\le p_*$ meets the final requirements. The set of interior slot points on a fixed finite grid is finite. Induct on the number of these points missing from $E$. When none is missing there is no disjoint nonempty test, so $\mathsf I_p(E)$ holds vacuously.

Assume the assertion for pin sets with fewer missing grid points than $E$, and let $T$ be a test for $E$. Since $T$ is nonempty and disjoint from $E$, the set $P=E\cup T$ has strictly fewer missing points. The induction hypothesis therefore supplies $\mathsf I_p(P)$, exactly the hypothesis used in Proposition <a href="#prop:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:regularity">20</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:leakage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:leakage">30</a>. The two leakage estimates and Lemma <a href="#lem:two-ends" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:two-ends">27</a> give $$\mathbb E_P\langle f,(I-U)f\rangle
                         \ge m(1-\varepsilon(p)).$$ By Lemma <a href="#lem:return-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:return-energy">16</a>, or <a href="#eq:leakage-hitting-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:leakage-hitting-input">[eq:leakage-hitting-input]</a>, it follows that $r\le\varepsilon(p)$. Proposition <a href="#prop:pin-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pin-test">6</a> now yields $$\mathbb P_E(T\subseteq\mathcal B_E)
        \le(2r)^m\le(2\varepsilon(p))^m\le p^m.$$ This proves the induction step for every allowed $T$, and finiteness of the grid completes the induction. A singleton interior slot test at time zero satisfies the growth bound, so taking $E=D_0$ gives <a href="#eq:singleton-missing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:singleton-missing">[eq:singleton-missing]</a>. A boundary slot belongs to $D_0$, and its missing-cycle probability is zero. This covers all slots. ◻

</details>

Theorem <a href="#thm:pin-bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:pin-bootstrap">31</a> supplies the uniform missing-cycle bound required for the magnetization argument. The grid and the larger pin sets were devices for a finite induction; the conclusion <a href="#eq:singleton-missing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:singleton-missing">[eq:singleton-missing]</a> concerns the original continuous boundary pins alone.

<a id="sec:kms"></a>
### From pinned loops to a magnetized equilibrium state

Theorem <a href="#thm:pin-bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:pin-bootstrap">31</a> says that, under boundary pinning, each slot has small probability of belonging to a cycle that misses the boundary. We now turn that statement into a state for the original zero-field dynamics. First we obtain a tail bound for the exact spectral distribution of total magnetization in a free box. A field tending to zero selects that tail, and translation averages produce a state with positive magnetization. The last step verifies both boundary values of the KMS strip for that same state.

Fix $d\geq3$ and $S\in\{\tfrac12,1,\tfrac32,\ldots\}$. Let $p_*$ be the threshold supplied by Theorem <a href="#thm:pin-bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:pin-bootstrap">31</a> for $\ell=2S$, and put $p=p_*$ and $\beta_0(d,S)=5p_*^{-11/10}$. Throughout this Section, $\beta\geq\beta_0(d,S)$ is fixed and finite. Write $$\Lambda_N=[-N,N]^d\cap\mathbb Z^d,\qquad
 V_N=|\Lambda_N|,\qquad
 M_N=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_N}S_x^z,\qquad H_N=H_{\Lambda_N}.$$ The quantity $V_N$ here is the number of sites; the slot set remains $V_{\Lambda_N}=\Lambda_N\times\{1,\ldots,\ell\}$, with $\ell=2S$. Let $\mu_N$ be the spectral distribution of $M_N$ in the free zero-field Gibbs state: $$\mu_N(I)=
 \frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\bigl(e^{-\beta H_N}\mathbf 1_I(M_N)\bigr)}
      {\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(e^{-\beta H_N})}
 \quad\text{for Borel sets }I\subset\mathbb R.$$ By Proposition <a href="#prop:spin-loops" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:spin-loops">3</a>, this is exactly the time-zero magnetization distribution in the cycle-coloring representation.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### A spectral tail with surface-order cost

<a id="stmt-lem-surface-cost"></a>
<div id="lem:surface-cost" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:boundary-color-cost"></a>
<a id="eq:free-spectral-tail"></a>
**Lemma 32** (Boundary coloring and a spectral tail). *For every $N\geq1$, let $G_N$ be the event in the zero-field mark-and-color law that every cycle meeting $D_0$ is colored up. There is a constant $C_{\beta,d,S}<\infty$, independent of $N$, such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \Pr(G_N)&\geq
       \exp\{-C_{\beta,d,S}|\partial\Lambda_N|\},
       \label{eq:boundary-color-cost}\\
 \mu_N([SV_N/2,\infty))&\geq
       \tfrac12\exp\{-C_{\beta,d,S}|\partial\Lambda_N|\}.
       \label{eq:free-spectral-tail}
\end{aligned}$$ Conditionally on $G_N$, the uncolored picture has law $\mathbb P_{D_0}$, and the cycles missing $D_0$ still have independent fair colors.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-surface-cost"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-surface-cost</summary>

*Proof.* Let $n_0$ be the number of cycles meeting $D_0$, and let $J_N$ count inter-site marks incident to a boundary site during one period. Every cycle meeting the boundary either contains a time-zero boundary slot or uses an inter-site mark incident to the boundary. Indeed, in the absence of a time-zero boundary slot on that cycle, a boundary visit must begin by a spatial jump; within-site endpoint permutations do not change the site. Each mark has two strands and can witness at most two cycles. Consequently, picture by picture, $$n_0\leq \ell|\partial\Lambda_N|+2J_N.$$ There are at most $2d\ell^2|\partial\Lambda_N|$ slot edges incident to the boundary. Their base rate is $1/2$. The first factorial bound of Lemma <a href="#lem:factorial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:factorial">5</a>, applied with no pins, therefore gives $$\mathbb E_{\varnothing}J_N
 \leq 2d\beta\ell^2|\partial\Lambda_N|,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E_{\varnothing}n_0
 \leq(\ell+4d\beta\ell^2)|\partial\Lambda_N|.$$ Given a picture, $G_N$ has probability $2^{-n_0}$. Jensen’s inequality now proves <a href="#eq:boundary-color-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boundary-color-cost">[eq:boundary-color-cost]</a>, for example with $C_{\beta,d,S}=(\log2)(\ell+4d\beta\ell^2)$.

Multiplying the zero-field picture weight $2^{\#\mathrm{cycles}}$ by $2^{-n_0}$ leaves precisely the weight for cycles missing $D_0$. This proves the assertion about the conditional picture; conditioning fixes only the colors of the other cycles. For a time-zero slot $i$, its conditional mean spin is thus $$\frac12\bigl(1-\mathbb P_{D_0}(i\in\mathcal B_{D_0})\bigr).$$ For an unpinned slot the probability on the right is at most $p$ by the singleton case of Theorem <a href="#thm:pin-bootstrap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:pin-bootstrap">31</a>; for a boundary slot it is zero. Summing over the $\ell V_N$ slots gives $$\mathbb E\left[\frac{M_N}{V_N}\,\middle|\,G_N\right]
       \geq S(1-p).$$ Here $M_N$ denotes the random variable in the coloring representation. If $q=\Pr(M_N/V_N\geq S/2\mid G_N)$, its upper bound $M_N/V_N\leq S$ implies $$S(1-p)\leq Sq+\frac S2(1-q),\qquad q\geq1-2p>\frac12.$$ Multiply by $\Pr(G_N)$ and use the exact spectral interpretation of the unconditioned coloring law to obtain <a href="#eq:free-spectral-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-spectral-tail">[eq:free-spectral-tail]</a>. ◻

</details>

The boundary event has not changed the Hamiltonian whose spectral law appears in <a href="#eq:free-spectral-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-spectral-tail">[eq:free-spectral-tail]</a>. It supplies a tail event in the free Gibbs state whose logarithmic cost is of order the boundary size. We next select this tail by a homogeneous field whose volume contribution is larger than that cost, although the field itself tends to zero. The relation between the zero-field magnetization distribution and field-selected spontaneous magnetization goes back to Griffiths (Griffiths 1966) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[Griffiths1966](#ref-Griffiths1966)]; see also (Dyson, Lieb, and Simon 1978, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[DysonLiebSimon1978](#ref-DysonLiebSimon1978)]. Here the surface-order tail permits the explicit volume-dependent field used below.

<a id="stmt-prop-magnetized-limit"></a>
<div id="prop:magnetized-limit" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:positive-limit-magnetization"></a>
**Proposition 33** (An explicit vanishing-field limit). *For $N\geq1$, set $$h_N=N^{-1/2},\qquad K_N=H_N-h_NM_N,
 \qquad
 \rho_N^{\mathrm{in}}(C)=
   \frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(e^{-\beta K_N}C)}{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(e^{-\beta K_N})}
 \quad(C\in\mathcal A_{\Lambda_N}).$$ Extend $\rho_N^{\mathrm{in}}$ to a state $\rho_N$ on $\mathcal A$ by the product normalized trace outside $\Lambda_N$, and define <a id="eq:translation-average"></a>$$\label{eq:translation-average}
 \omega_N(A)=\frac1{V_N}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_N}\rho_N(T_x(A)).$$ There is a subsequence $N_j\to\infty$ on which $\omega_{N_j}$ converges pointwise on $\mathcal A$ to a translation-invariant state $\omega$, and every such subsequential limit satisfies $$\label{eq:positive-limit-magnetization}
 \omega(S_0^z)\geq S/4.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-magnetized-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-magnetized-limit</summary>

<a id="eq:field-tail"></a>
<a id="eq:field-mean"></a>
*Proof.* Each interaction term commutes with the sum of the two endpoint $z$-spins. This follows directly from $$\mathbf S_x\cdot\mathbf S_y
 =S_x^zS_y^z+\tfrac12(S_x^+S_y^-+S_x^-S_y^+).$$ Thus $[H_N,M_N]=0$. For any $h>0$, the spectral law in the Gibbs state of $H_N-hM_N$ is exactly $$\mu_{N,h}(\,\mathrm dm)
 =\frac{e^{\beta hm}\mu_N(\,\mathrm dm)}
        {\int e^{\beta hu}\mu_N(\,\mathrm du)}.$$ Bound the numerator on $m<SV_N/4$, and retain only $m\geq SV_N/2$ in the denominator. Lemma <a href="#lem:surface-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:surface-cost">32</a> then gives $$\label{eq:field-tail}
 \mu_{N,h}(({-\infty},SV_N/4))
 \leq 2\exp\left\{
       C_{\beta,d,S}|\partial\Lambda_N|-\frac{\beta hS}{4}V_N
       \right\}.$$ For these cubes, $$\frac{|\partial\Lambda_N|}{V_N}
 =1-\left(\frac{2N-1}{2N+1}\right)^d
 \leq\frac{2d}{2N+1}.$$ At $h=h_N$, the exponent in <a href="#eq:field-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:field-tail">[eq:field-tail]</a> tends to $-\infty$: its negative term has order $N^{d-1/2}$, whereas its positive term has order $N^{d-1}$. Hence, writing $q_N=\mu_{N,h_N}(({-\infty},SV_N/4))$, we have $q_N\to0$. The spectral bounds $-S\leq M_N/V_N\leq S$ imply $$\label{eq:field-mean}
 \rho_N^{\mathrm{in}}(M_N/V_N)
 \geq\frac S4(1-q_N)-Sq_N
 =\frac S4-\frac{5S}{4}q_N.$$ In particular this estimate uses a single explicit volume-field sequence for the fixed $\beta$.

Choose a countable norm-dense set in the union of local matrix algebras, closed under rational complex linear combinations and adjoints. The bound $|\omega_N(A)|\leq\|A\|$ permits diagonal subsequence selection on that set. Norm approximation then gives pointwise convergence on $\mathcal A$. The resulting functional $\omega$ is linear, normalized and positive, since these properties hold for each $\omega_N$ and pass to pointwise limits. It is therefore a state.

For every fixed $y\in\mathbb Z^d$ and $A\in\mathcal A$, $$|\omega_N(T_y(A))-\omega_N(A)|
 \leq\frac{|(\Lambda_N+y)\mathbin\triangle\Lambda_N|}{V_N}\|A\|
 \longrightarrow0.$$ This proves translation invariance of $\omega$. Finally, $$\omega_N(S_0^z)
 =\frac1{V_N}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_N}\rho_N(S_x^z)
 =\rho_N^{\mathrm{in}}(M_N/V_N).$$ Taking the limit in <a href="#eq:field-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:field-mean">[eq:field-mean]</a> proves <a href="#eq:positive-limit-magnetization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-limit-magnetization">[eq:positive-limit-magnetization]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="subsec:deep-dynamics"></a>
#### Dynamics far from the translated boundary

Fix from now on one state subsequence $N_j$ provided by Proposition <a href="#prop:magnetized-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:magnetized-limit">33</a>. We will prove the KMS condition along this same subsequence for every local pair; no further subsequence depending on that pair will be taken. The needed dynamical approximation must hold uniformly over translations whose distance from the box boundary diverges.

We first check the hypotheses of the dynamics existence theorem cited in Section <a href="#sec:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:model">1.1</a>, (Nachtergaele, Ogata, and Sims 2006, Theorem 2.2). Use the lattice graph metric and the summable function $F(r)=(1+r)^{-d-1}$, which has the convolution bound required there. The interaction is supported on nearest-neighbor pairs, each term has norm at most $3S^2$, and every site belongs to $2d$ such pairs. Thus its interaction norm in that theorem is finite, with parameter $a=0$.

For a finite cube $\Gamma$, write $\tau_t^\Gamma(B)=e^{itH_\Gamma}Be^{-itH_\Gamma}$, acting trivially outside $\Gamma$. Likewise let $$\alpha_z^N(B)=e^{izK_N}Be^{-izK_N}\qquad(z\in\mathbb C).$$ For real $t$, these maps are isometric automorphisms. For complex $z$, $\alpha_z^N$ is defined on the entire quasi-local algebra because $K_N$ is a bounded local operator.

<a id="stmt-lem-deep-dynamics"></a>
<div id="lem:deep-dynamics" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:deep-box-zero-field"></a>
<a id="eq:deep-box-with-field"></a>
**Lemma 34** (Uniform approximation in deep translated boxes). *Let $B$ be a local observable, let $L$ be a finite set containing $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}B$ and the origin, and let $0<T<\infty$. Then $$\label{eq:deep-box-zero-field}
 \lim_{r\to\infty}\ 
 \sup_{\substack{\Gamma\text{ a finite cube}\\
                 \mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_\infty(L,\Gamma^c)\geq r}}
 \ \sup_{|t|\leq T}\|\tau_t^\Gamma(B)-\tau_t(B)\|=0.$$ Here $\Gamma^c=\mathbb Z^d\setminus\Gamma$. If $$I_N=\{x\in\Lambda_N:
       \mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_\infty(x,\Lambda_N^c)\geq\lfloor\sqrt N\rfloor\},$$ then $|\Lambda_N\setminus I_N|/V_N\to0$ and $$\label{eq:deep-box-with-field}
 \lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{x\in I_N}\sup_{|t|\leq T}
 \|\alpha_t^N(T_x(B))-T_x(\tau_t(B))\|=0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-deep-dynamics"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-deep-dynamics</summary>

*Proof.* First fix $t$. If <a href="#eq:deep-box-zero-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:deep-box-zero-field">[eq:deep-box-zero-field]</a> failed at that time, there would be $\varepsilon>0$ and cubes $\Gamma_k$ whose distances from $L$ to their complements tend to infinity, with $\|\tau_t^{\Gamma_k}(B)-\tau_t(B)\|\geq\varepsilon$. Such cubes contain centered boxes with radii tending to infinity. We can therefore select a subsequence that is increasing by inclusion and exhausts $\mathbb Z^d$: after any finite selected cube, a sufficiently late cube contains it and the next prescribed centered box. This contradicts the norm convergence along increasing boxes in the definition of $\tau_t$.

To make this convergence uniform in time, write $\Phi(\{u,v\})=-\mathbf S_u\cdot\mathbf S_v$. Only edges meeting $L$ contribute to $[H_\Gamma,B]$, so for every $\Gamma$ $$\|[H_\Gamma,B]\|\leq
 2\|B\|\sum_{\substack{e\text{ nearest-neighbor edge}\\e\cap L\ne\varnothing}}
                   \|\Phi(e)\|=:c_B<\infty.$$ Consequently $\|\tau_t^\Gamma(B)-\tau_u^\Gamma(B)\|\leq c_B|t-u|$. Passing to the infinite-volume limit gives the same bound for $\tau_t(B)$. A finite mesh of $[-T,T]$, together with the fixed-time conclusion, proves <a href="#eq:deep-box-zero-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:deep-box-zero-field">[eq:deep-box-zero-field]</a>.

For $x\in I_N$, set $\Gamma=\Lambda_N-x$. Then $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}_\infty(L,\Gamma^c)\to\infty$ uniformly over these $x$, since $L$ is fixed. For all sufficiently large $N$, $L\subset\Gamma$. Define the local rotation $$\gamma_\theta^L(B)
   =e^{i\theta\sum_{u\in L}S_u^z}B
      e^{-i\theta\sum_{u\in L}S_u^z}.$$ The commutation $[H_N,M_N]=0$ and translation of the finite Hamiltonian give the exact formula <a id="eq:field-rotation-sign"></a>$$\label{eq:field-rotation-sign}
 T_{-x}\bigl(\alpha_t^N(T_x(B))\bigr)
       =\tau_t^\Gamma\bigl(\gamma_{-h_Nt}^L(B)\bigr).$$ The norm effect of this rotation is bounded by $$\|\gamma_{-h_Nt}^L(B)-B\|
 \leq h_N|t|\left\|\left[\sum_{u\in L}S_u^z,B\right]\right\|.$$ Use the isometry of $\tau_t^\Gamma$, followed by <a href="#eq:deep-box-zero-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:deep-box-zero-field">[eq:deep-box-zero-field]</a>, to prove <a href="#eq:deep-box-with-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:deep-box-with-field">[eq:deep-box-with-field]</a>. Finally, the excluded sites lie in a layer of thickness at most $\lfloor\sqrt N\rfloor$, whose fraction of the cube is $O(N^{-1/2})$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### The analytic strip and its two boundary values

We will use the following elementary strip lemma. Its first part establishes a common strip bound from finite, possibly nonuniform interior bounds. Only after obtaining that common bound does its second part use Gaussian decay to control convergence on the whole boundary.

<a id="stmt-lem-strip-limit"></a>
<div id="lem:strip-limit" class="lemma">

**Lemma 35** (Bounded strip limits). *Fix $\beta>0$, and put $\mathcal S_\beta=\{z\in\mathbb C:0\leq\operatorname{Im}z\leq\beta\}$. Let $(F_n)_{n\geq1}$ be functions continuous on $\mathcal S_\beta$, analytic in its interior, and individually bounded there. Suppose a constant $C\geq0$ satisfies $$|F_n(t)|\leq C,\qquad |F_n(t+i\beta)|\leq C
 \quad(n\geq1,\ t\in\mathbb R).$$ Then $|F_n(z)|\leq C$ throughout $\mathcal S_\beta$. If the two boundary sequences converge locally uniformly on $\mathbb R$ to $f_0$ and $f_\beta$, respectively, then the full sequence $F_n$ converges locally uniformly on the closed strip to a bounded function $F$, continuous on the closed strip and analytic in its interior, with $$|F(z)|\leq C,\qquad F(t)=f_0(t),\qquad F(t+i\beta)=f_\beta(t).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-strip-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-strip-limit</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $n$, and let $M_n<\infty$ bound $|F_n|$ on the strip. For $\varepsilon>0$, apply the maximum principle to $e^{-\varepsilon z^2}F_n(z)$ on $[-R,R]+i[0,\beta]$. Its modulus is at most $Ce^{\varepsilon\beta^2}$ on the horizontal sides and at most $M_ne^{-\varepsilon R^2+\varepsilon\beta^2}$ on the vertical sides. Send $R\to\infty$ while holding a point $z$ fixed, then send $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. This proves $|F_n(z)|\leq C$. Only the already assumed finite bound $M_n$ was needed to remove the vertical sides.

Now set $G_n(z)=e^{-z^2}F_n(z)$. The common bound just proved implies $$|G_n(t+iy)|\leq Ce^{-t^2+y^2}
       \quad(0\leq y\leq\beta).$$ Local uniform convergence of the two boundary sequences and the Gaussian tail therefore make $(G_n)$ uniformly Cauchy on both horizontal edges. Write $$\delta_{n,m}=
 \max_{y\in\{0,\beta\}}\sup_{t\in\mathbb R}
           |G_n(t+iy)-G_m(t+iy)|;
 \qquad \delta_{n,m}\longrightarrow0.$$ The maximum principle on the same rectangles, now applied to $G_n-G_m$, has horizontal bound $\delta_{n,m}$ and vertical bound $2Ce^{-R^2+\beta^2}$. Letting $R\to\infty$ shows $$\sup_{z\in\mathcal S_\beta}|G_n(z)-G_m(z)|\leq\delta_{n,m}.$$ Hence $G_n$ converges uniformly on the closed strip to a continuous function $G$, analytic in the interior. Multiplication by $e^{z^2}$ gives local uniform convergence of $F_n$ on the closed strip to $F=e^{z^2}G$. Its claimed boundary values and the bound $|F|\leq C$ follow by taking pointwise limits. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-strip-limit-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-strip-limit</summary>

<a id="eq:finite-strip-functions"></a>
<a id="eq:finite-top-edge"></a>
*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Let $\omega$ and $N_j$ be as in Proposition <a href="#prop:magnetized-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:magnetized-limit">33</a>. For local observables $A,B$, define the entire functions $$\label{eq:finite-strip-functions}
 F_N^{A,B}(z)=\frac1{V_N}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_N}
       \rho_N\bigl(T_x(A)\alpha_z^N(T_x(B))\bigr).$$ Trace cyclicity gives both identities <a id="eq:finite-bottom-edge"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 F_N^{A,B}(t)
 &=\frac1{V_N}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_N}
       \rho_N\bigl(T_x(A)\alpha_t^N(T_x(B))\bigr),
       \label{eq:finite-bottom-edge}\\
 F_N^{A,B}(t+i\beta)
 &=\frac1{V_N}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_N}
       \rho_N\bigl(\alpha_t^N(T_x(B))T_x(A)\bigr).
       \label{eq:finite-top-edge}
\end{aligned}$$ These formulas also hold when the translated supports cross the boundary of $\Lambda_N$. To see this without a support restriction, take a finite set containing $\Lambda_N$ and both translated supports. On its matrix algebra the density of $\rho_N$ is $e^{-\beta K_N}/\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(e^{-\beta K_N})$ tensored with a normalized identity. Equivalently, it is the Gibbs density for the operator $K_N\otimes I$ on that larger algebra. The dynamics is generated by that same operator, so finite-dimensional trace cyclicity proves <a href="#eq:finite-top-edge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-top-edge">[eq:finite-top-edge]</a> there as well.

For real $t$, the isometry of $\alpha_t^N$ bounds both right-hand sides by $\|A\|\|B\|$. Before using any common strip bound, we also have the finite bound $$|F_N^{A,B}(t+iy)|
 \leq \|A\|\|B\|e^{2\beta\|K_N\|}
 \quad(t\in\mathbb R,\ 0\leq y\leq\beta).$$ Indeed, the real-time unitary factors have norm one, and $\|e^{\pm yK_N}\|\leq e^{\beta\|K_N\|}$. The first part of Lemma <a href="#lem:strip-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:strip-limit">35</a> therefore applies and gives the uniform bound <a id="eq:strip-product-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:strip-product-bound}
 |F_N^{A,B}(z)|\leq\|A\|\|B\|
       \quad(z\in\mathcal S_\beta,\ N\geq1).$$

We next identify the two boundary limits. Given $0<T<\infty$, Lemma <a href="#lem:deep-dynamics" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:deep-dynamics">34</a> supplies numbers $\varepsilon_N(T)\to0$ bounding the norm error in <a href="#eq:deep-box-with-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:deep-box-with-field">[eq:deep-box-with-field]</a> on $I_N$. On its complement the error is at most $2\|B\|$, since both real-time dynamics are isometric. It follows that, uniformly for $|t|\leq T$, <a id="eq:bottom-edge-error"></a><a id="eq:top-edge-error"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \left|F_N^{A,B}(t)-\omega_N(A\tau_t(B))\right|
 &\leq\|A\|\varepsilon_N(T)
   +2\|A\|\|B\|\frac{|\Lambda_N\setminus I_N|}{V_N},
       \label{eq:bottom-edge-error}\\
 \left|F_N^{A,B}(t+i\beta)-\omega_N(\tau_t(B)A)\right|
 &\leq\|A\|\varepsilon_N(T)
   +2\|A\|\|B\|\frac{|\Lambda_N\setminus I_N|}{V_N}.
       \label{eq:top-edge-error}
\end{aligned}$$ For example, the comparison term in the first line is exactly $V_N^{-1}\sum_x\rho_N(T_x(A)T_x(\tau_t(B)))$, which equals $\omega_N(A\tau_t(B))$; the second line retains the opposite operator order. Thus both error bounds tend to zero.

<a id="eq:local-kms-bound"></a>
The maps $t\mapsto A\tau_t(B)$ and $t\mapsto\tau_t(B)A$ are norm-continuous. Their images of a compact time interval are norm-compact. Pointwise convergence $\omega_{N_j}(C)\to\omega(C)$ is uniform over each such image: use a finite norm net and the common functional norm one. Therefore, along the already chosen subsequence, $$\begin{aligned}
 F_{N_j}^{A,B}(t)&\longrightarrow\omega(A\tau_t(B)),\\
 F_{N_j}^{A,B}(t+i\beta)&\longrightarrow\omega(\tau_t(B)A)
\end{aligned}$$ locally uniformly for real $t$. Lemma <a href="#lem:strip-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:strip-limit">35</a> now gives a bounded continuous function $F_{A,B}$ on the closed strip, analytic in its interior, with precisely these two boundary values and $$\label{eq:local-kms-bound}
 \sup_{z\in\mathcal S_\beta}|F_{A,B}(z)|\leq\|A\|\|B\|.$$ Since the strip limit uses the full state subsequence, the bilinearity of <a href="#eq:finite-strip-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-strip-functions">[eq:finite-strip-functions]</a> passes to $F_{A,B}$.

It remains to obtain the asserted condition on the entire algebra. Let $A_n,B_n$ be local observables converging in norm to arbitrary $A,B\in\mathcal A$. Bilinearity and <a href="#eq:local-kms-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-kms-bound">[eq:local-kms-bound]</a> imply $$\sup_{z\in\mathcal S_\beta}
       |F_{A_n,B_n}(z)-F_{A_m,B_m}(z)|
 \leq \|A_n-A_m\|\|B_n\|
      +\|A_m\|\|B_n-B_m\|.$$ Hence these functions converge uniformly on the closed strip to a bounded continuous $F_{A,B}$, analytic in the interior. The same inequality proves independence of the approximating sequences and the bound $\sup|F_{A,B}|\leq\|A\|\|B\|$. Since states have norm one and $\tau_t$ is isometric, norm approximation passes both boundary identities to the limit, uniformly in real $t$: $$F_{A,B}(t)=\omega(A\tau_t(B)),\qquad
 F_{A,B}(t+i\beta)=\omega(\tau_t(B)A).$$ This is the full $\beta$-KMS condition <a href="#eq:kms-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kms-definition">[eq:kms-definition]</a> for the zero-field dynamics $\tau$ defined in Section <a href="#sec:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:model">1.1</a>. The state is translation invariant and satisfies $\omega(S_0^z)\geq S/4>0$ by Proposition <a href="#prop:magnetized-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:magnetized-limit">33</a>. Since $\beta$ was any fixed finite number at least $\beta_0(d,S)$, Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> follows. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The first-moment reduction at a fixed exposure and a finite set $J$ of pins on selected time cuts, with $ℚ_{a}(G_{J}) > 0$. The exterior-moment identity is used for the unconditioned law in the upper-left box; Proposition 14 proves that the two routes to the lower-right box agree. The lower-left walk resamples its transitions, whereas the upper-right law follows a single sampled permutation. The equality concerns the next-pin mean and does not identify these two path laws. When the exposure is averaged, its actual marginal still contains the factor $g_{P}(a)$ in [eq:exposure-outer-law].",
      "figure_id": "fig:exposure-first-moment",
      "pdf_page": 22,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:exposure-first-moment",
          "exact_quote": "The first-moment reduction at a fixed exposure and a finite set $J$\nof pins on selected time cuts, with $\\mathbb Q_{\\mathbf a}(G_J)>0$.  The exterior-moment identity is used for the unconditioned\nlaw in the upper-left box; Proposition~\\ref{",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  box/.style={draw=blue!45!black,rounded corners=2pt,fill=blue!3,\n              align=center,text width=5.2cm,minimum height=1.3cm,inner sep=6pt},\n  >={Stealth[length=2mm]},font=\\small]\n  \\node[box] (independent) at (0,0)\n    {Unconditioned hole picture $\\mathbb Q_{\\mathbf a}$\\\\\n     $K=\\mathbb E_{\\mathbb Q_{\\mathbf a}}\\Pi$};\n  \\node[box] (conditioned) at (7,0)\n    {Conditioned hole picture\\\\\n     $\\mathbb Q_{\\mathbf a}(\\,\\cdot\\mid G_J)$};\n  \\node[box] (walk) at (0,-2.6)\n    {Markov walk with transition $K$\\\\\n     First positive $J$-hitting kernel};\n  \\node[box] (moment) at (7,-2.6)\n    {Conditional next-pin mean\\\\\n     $\\mathbb E[\\Sigma\\mid G_J]$};\n  \\draw[->,thick] (independent) -- node[above,align=center]\n    {condition\\\\on $G_J$} (conditioned);\n  \\draw[->,thick] (independent) -- node[left,align=right]\n    {retain the\\\\one-step mean} (walk);\n  \\draw[->,thick] (conditioned) -- node[right,align=left]\n    {retain the\\\\next-pin mean} (moment);\n  \\draw[<->,thick] (walk) -- node[below,align=center]\n    {equal\\\\kernels} (moment);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Commutative diagram with four rectangular nodes in a 2x2 grid connected by directed arrows:\n- Upper-left box: contains \"Unconditioned hole picture $\\mathbb{Q}_{\\mathbf{a}}$\" and \"$K = \\mathbb{E}_{\\mathbb{Q}_{\\mathbf{a}}} \\Pi$\".\n- Upper-right box: contains \"Conditioned hole picture\" and \"$\\mathbb{Q}_{\\mathbf{a}}(\\cdot \\mid G_J)$\".\n- Lower-left box: contains \"Markov walk with transition $K$\" and \"First positive $J$-hitting kernel\".\n- Lower-right box: contains \"Conditional next-pin mean\" and \"$\\mathbb{E}[\\Sigma \\mid G_J]$\".\n- Horizontal arrow from upper-left to upper-right labeled \"condition\" on top and \"on $G_J$\" below.\n- Vertical arrow from upper-left to lower-left labeled on the left \"retain the\" (top) and \"one-step mean\" (bottom).\n- Vertical arrow from upper-right to lower-right labeled on the right \"retain the\" (top) and \"next-pin mean\" (bottom).\n- Horizontal arrow from lower-left to lower-right labeled \"equal\" on top and \"kernels\" below."
    },
    {
      "caption": "Replacing lattice edges by surviving paths. In (a), a two-dimensional example, $C = {z : |z|_{∞} = 6}$ is a blocked ring. Its radius-four thickening is $A_{C} = {z : 2 ≤ |z|_{∞} ≤ 10}$; filling the surviving pocket $B_{1}(0)$ gives $H_{C} = B_{10}(0)$. The original edge $u → v$, with $u = (10,0)$ and $v = (11,0)$, is replaced by the indicated path from $r_{H} = (0,11)$ to $v$. The open corner is a surviving interpolation vertex outside $S_{C}$: a nearest-neighbor route need not remain in $S_{C}$ itself. In (b), the only blocked sites are $0$ and $9e_{1}$. Their filled radius-four hulls are the two displayed lattice cubes, shown schematically in a coordinate slice; the transverse scale is compressed. Both $u = 4e_{1}$ and $v = 5e_{1}$ survive. Choosing $r_{H0} = v$ and $r_{H1} = u$ sends the oriented original edge $u → v$ to the reverse edge $v → u$. This is the case of [eq:adjacent-hull-route] in which the two boundary portions are empty. Applying $ρ$ again would undo this required image exchange. Panel (a) illustrates the definitions in $d = 2$; the hull and boundary properties in every $d ≥ 2$ are proved in Lemma 23.",
      "figure_id": "fig:hull-routing",
      "pdf_page": 34,

          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
